Canonical integral models of Shimura varieties of abelian type
Patrick Daniels, Alex Youcis

Abstract
. We prove a conjecture of Pappas and Rapoport for all Shimura varieties of abelian type with parahoric level structure when p > 3 p>3 by showing that the Kisin–Pappas–Zhou integral models of Shimura varieties of abelian type are canonical. In particular, this shows that these models of are independent of the choices made during their construction, and that they satisfy functoriality with respect to morphisms of Shimura data.



1.
Introduction

In
[
42
]
, Pappas and Rapoport conjecture the existence of “canonical” integral models with parahoric level structure and establish this conjecture in most cases of Hodge type. In this work, we prove the conjecture of Pappas and Rapoport for (almost all) Shimura varieties of abelian type. In particular, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem A

(See Theorem
4.10
)
.

Let
p>3
. Then the Pappas–Rapoport conjecture holds for Shimura varieties of abelian type with parahoric level structure at
p
.

Let us begin to contextualize this result. Shimura varieties are algebro-geometric objects which sit at the intersection of number theory and representation theory. They take the form of a projective system
\{\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\}_{{\sf K}}
, where
\mathbf{G}
is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbf{X}
is some auxiliary Hodge-theoretic datum, and
{\sf K}\subseteq\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
ranges over (neat) compact open subgroups. Shimura varieties have been inextricably linked with the Langlands program from its inception, as the cohomology of
\{\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\}_{{\sf K}}
ought to realize the global Langlands conjecture for
\mathbf{G}
(see e.g.,
[
24
]
). Central to this cohomological understanding of the Langlands program is a conjecture which posits a “motivic decomposition” of the
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-points of certain “canonical” integral models
\{\mathscr{S}_{\sf K}\}_{{\sf K}}
of
\{\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\}_{{\sf K}}
(see
[
33
]
). It is then not surprising that the construction of such natural integral models of Shimura varieties has prominently featured in many of the advances in the Langlands program.

Constructing such canonical integral models, and even characterizing what constitutes a “canonical” integral model, is quite difficult. Despite their importance, Shimura varieties in general remain fairly inexplicit in their definition. The variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
ought to parameterize
\mathbf{G}^{c}
-motives
M
of type
\mathbf{X}
with
{\sf K}
-level structure on the étale realization of
M
(e.g., see
[
36
]
). Here
\mathbf{G}^{c}
is a certain quotient of
\mathbf{G}
, which agrees with
\mathbf{G}
in the Hodge-type case, but differs from
\mathbf{G}
in some abelian-type cases. While technical in nature, consideration of
\mathbf{G}^{c}
is crucial for such a conjectural motivic interpretation. Unfortunately, such a motivic description is currently out of reach, and instead
\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is constructed from various abstract algebro-geometric existence results, which are not amenable to the study of integral models.

Despite this, Pappas and Rapoport in
[
42
]
(building off previous work of Pappas in
[
41
]
) made significant headway in a definition of such conjectural canonical integral models when
{\sf K}={\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}
where
{\sf K}_{p}=\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is a parahoric subgroup of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. This parahoricity-at-
p
condition may be thought of as representing the situation where relatively little level structure is imposed at
p
.

To explicate their work, let
E
be the completion of the reflex field of
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
at a place
v
lying over
p
. Pappas and Rapoport call a system
\{\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\}_{{\sf K}^{p}}
of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-models of
\{\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{E}\}_{{\sf K}^{p}}

canonical
if

(1)

the transition maps between the varying levels are finite étale,

(2)

if
R
is a characteristic
(0,p)
discrete valuation ring over
\mathcal{O}_{E}
then

\displaystyle\left(\varprojlim_{{\sf K}^{p}}\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\right)(R[\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}])=\left(\varprojlim_{{\sf K}^{p}}\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\right)(R),

(3)

there exists a
\mathcal{G}^{c}
-shtuka on
\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
modeling the étale realization functor on
\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{E}
,

(4)

and for every
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point
x
of
\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
, there is an isomorphism

(1.1)

\displaystyle\Theta_{x}:\left(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G}^{c},b_{x},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.65}{$\displaystyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\textstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.24472pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.88196pt\mu\hskip-4.31203pt\raisebox{-2.0675pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-2.03839pt\raisebox{1.85655pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 2.25403pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-0.88908pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.62997pt\mu\hskip-3.08002pt\raisebox{-1.47679pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-1.456pt\raisebox{1.32611pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 1.61002pt$}{}}$}}_{h}^{c}}}\right)^{\wedge}_{/x_{0}}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\left(\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})^{\wedge}_{/x}\right)^{\lozenge},

such that
\Theta_{x}^{\ast}(\mathscr{P}_{\sf K})=\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{univ}}
.

Here the étale realization is a certain
\mathcal{G}^{c}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-local system on
\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
which should be thought of as the étale realization of the “universal motive” on
\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
, but whose definition is unconditional on such a motivic interpretation. The group
\mathcal{G}^{c}
is a parahoric
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-model for
\mathbf{G}^{c}
which is determined from
\mathcal{G}
using Bruhat–Tits theory. Furthermore, when we speak of shtukas here we mean in the sense of
[
42
]
, and
\mathscr{P}_{{\sf K}^{p}}
modeling the étale realization is meant in terms of the construction in
[
42
, §2.6]
. Finally,
(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\mathcal{G}^{c},b_{x},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.65}{$\displaystyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\textstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.24472pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.88196pt\mu\hskip-4.31203pt\raisebox{-2.0675pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-2.03839pt\raisebox{1.85655pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 2.25403pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-0.88908pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.62997pt\mu\hskip-3.08002pt\raisebox{-1.47679pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-1.456pt\raisebox{1.32611pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 1.61002pt$}{}}$}}_{h}^{c}}})^{\wedge}_{/x_{0}}
is the completion of the integral local Shimura variety in the sense of
[
49
, Chapter 25]
with its universal shtuka
\mathscr{P}^{\mathrm{univ}}
. We refer the reader to §
4.1
for the definition of
b_{x}
and
x_{0}
, but mention that
{\mbox{$\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt$}{}_{h}^{c}}
is the geometric conjugacy class of cocharacters of
G^{c}
induced by the hodge cocharacter
\mbox{$\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt$}{}_{h}
of
G
, where we are writing
G=\mathbf{G}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
G^{c}=\mathbf{G}^{c}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

To help process this definition, note that, given the motivational moduli description of
\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
, it is reasonable to expect that
\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
should be a moduli space of
\mathcal{G}^{c}
-motives. One can view conditions (iii) and (iv) as saying that
\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
possesses a shtuka, which should be the “shtuka realization” of the universal
\mathcal{G}^{c}
-motive, which, while not provably universal, is universal everywhere formally locally. Furthermore, condition (i) is natural, and condition (ii) is a technical condition ensuring that the members of the system
\{\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\}_{{\sf K}^{p}}
have sufficiently large special fibers.

Pappas and Rapoport prove that canonical integral models are unique if they exist, hence they do provide a good notion of models at parahoric level. Constructing such models is difficult though, and seems out of reach for general Shimura varieties with current methods. That said, when
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is of so-called “abelian type”, the situation is much improved. Shimura varieties of abelian type may be thought of intuitively as moduli spaces of abelian motives with extra structure, but this again largely motivational (although see
[
36
, Theorem 11.16]
for a partial justification of this claim).

Building off the work of Kisin (and others), Kisin--Pappas and Kisin--Zhou have constructed integral models for Shimura varieties of abelian type at parahoric level
1
1

1

The construction of the Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models requires some minor additional assumptions when
p
is 3, see Remark
3.9
. These models have proven to be quite useful in applications (e.g., see
[
29
]
), but they have not yet been well-studied. For instance, there is no a priori functoriality for these models, and it is not even clear that the models are independent of the various ancillary choices made in their construction. Our main theorem clarifies these points.

Theorem B

(see Theorem
4.10
)
.

Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models are canonical.

One of the most pleasing consequences of this result is the following.

Corollary C

(See Corollary
4.12
and Theorem
4.14
)
.

Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models are independent of all choices, and are functorial in the triple
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
.

We expect that our main result will have applications beyond this functoriality. For example, in
[
9
]
, it is shown that Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models of Hodge type admit a fiber product decomposition
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})^{\diamond}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.65}{$\displaystyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\textstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.24472pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.88196pt\mu\hskip-4.31203pt\raisebox{-2.0675pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-2.03839pt\raisebox{1.85655pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 2.25403pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-0.88908pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.62997pt\mu\hskip-3.08002pt\raisebox{-1.47679pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-1.456pt\raisebox{1.32611pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 1.61002pt$}{}}$}}_{h}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Igs}_{{\sf K}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}

at the level of
v
-sheaves. Here
\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})^{\diamond}
denotes the
v
-sheaf associated to the
p
-adic completion of
\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
,
\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.65}{$\displaystyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\textstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.24472pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.88196pt\mu\hskip-4.31203pt\raisebox{-2.0675pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-2.03839pt\raisebox{1.85655pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 2.25403pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-0.88908pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.62997pt\mu\hskip-3.08002pt\raisebox{-1.47679pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-1.456pt\raisebox{1.32611pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 1.61002pt$}{}}$}}_{h}}
is the moduli
v
-stack of
\mathcal{G}
-shtukas bounded by
\mbox{$\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt$}{}_{h}
,
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is the stack of
G
-bundles on the Fargues-Fontaine curve, and
\mathrm{Igs}_{{\sf K}}
is the
Igusa stack
defined in
[
9
]
, extending work of Zhang
[
51
]
in the PEL-type case. Such a decomposition provides a crucial ingredient for the proof of torsion vanishing results for the cohomology of Shimura varieties, see
[
19
]
and
[
9
]
. Our results produce a map of
v
-sheaves
\mathscr{S}_{{\sf K}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})^{\diamond}\to\mathcal{G}^{c}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.65}{$\displaystyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\textstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.24472pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.88196pt\mu\hskip-4.31203pt\raisebox{-2.0675pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-2.03839pt\raisebox{1.85655pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 2.25403pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-0.88908pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.62997pt\mu\hskip-3.08002pt\raisebox{-1.47679pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-1.456pt\raisebox{1.32611pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 1.61002pt$}{}}$}}_{h}^{c}}
for all Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models, and we expect that with this it will be possible to extend the results of
[
51
]
and
[
9
]
to the abelian-type case.

Let us close by mentioning a few ideas in the proof of Theorem
4.10
. A useful heuristic for Shimura data of abelian type is that they are “spanned” by two (largely orthogonal) extremes: Shimura data of Hodge type and Shimura data of toral type. While a useful guiding principle, it requires some care to put this idea into practice. The main ingredient in our proof is the development of a precise formalism for realizing this heuristic. We do this by extending ideas of Lovering (see
[
31
]
) from the setting of Kisin’s canonical integral models at hyperspecial level to the setting of arbitrary Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models. We are then able to prove Theorem
A
by reducing to the Hodge-type case (established in
[
42
]
and
[
10
]
, see Remark
4.7
) and toral-type case (established in
[
7
]
). We expect these methods to have applications more generally.

Remark 1.1
.

We would like to direct the reader’s attention to
[
50
]
. In the process of this work, van Hoften and Sempliner have occasion to prove a modified version of Theorem
A
. Namely, they establish that Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models are “canonical” in an appropriate sense where one replaces
\mathcal{G}^{c}
with the more coarse quotient
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{ad}}
. Their proof avoids much of the most subtle group theory used in proving Theorem
A
, but it is still sufficient to establish Corollary
C
(see
[
50
]
). That said, the
\mathcal{G}^{\textup{ad}}
-version of Theorem
A
is insufficient for many “motivic” applications, such as extending the results of
[
9
]
to the abelian-type case.

Notation and conventions

•

For an
S
-scheme
X
, and a morphism of schemes
T\to S
, we shorten the notation
X\times_{S}T
to
X_{T}
. When
T=\textup{Spec}\,(R)
, we shorten this notation further to
X_{R}
. Similar conventions hold in other geometric categories.

•

For a scheme
X
and subset
S
, we always consider the Zariski closure
\overline{S}
as a reduced subscheme, unless stated otherwise.

•

Unless stated otherwise, we use the notation and terminology concerning
v
-sheaves, Fargues–Fontaine curve, and shtukas as in
[
42
, §2]
. In particular, when we speak of a shtuka over a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-scheme
X
, we mean a shtuka over the
v
-sheaf
X^{\lozenge/}
in the sense of
[
42
, Definition 2.3.2]
.

•

We use
\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(\mathscr{X})
and
\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.65}{$\displaystyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\textstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.77817pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.47495pt\mu\hskip-7.21117pt\raisebox{-2.95358pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-3.40889pt\raisebox{2.65222pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 3.7695pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-1.24472pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.88196pt\mu\hskip-4.31203pt\raisebox{-2.0675pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-2.03839pt\raisebox{1.85655pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 2.25403pt$}{}}$}}{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mbox{$\raisebox{-0.88908pt}{$l$}\hskip-0.62997pt\mu\hskip-3.08002pt\raisebox{-1.47679pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-1.456pt\raisebox{1.32611pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 1.61002pt$}{}}$}}}(\mathscr{X})
to denote the groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
-shtukas on
\mathscr{X}
and groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
-sthukas bounded by
\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt
on
\mathscr{X}
, respectively.

•

A reductive group over a field
F
is always assumed connected.

2.
Preliminaries

We begin by establishing some preliminaries which will be essential for the proofs of the main results. In particular, we review the Bruhat–Tits theory which is necessary for our purposes, and discuss torsors for fiber products of group schemes.

2.1.
Some basic Bruhat–Tits theory

In this section we review some basic Bruhat–Tits theory. For more in-depth discussion in this direction we suggest the reader to consult
[
6
]
or
[
27
]
and the references therein.

Throughout this section we fix a discretely valued field
K
with perfect residue field
k
. We denote by
K^{\mathrm{ur}}
the maximal unramified extension of
K
inside of
K^{\mathrm{sep}}
, and by
\Gamma
the Galois group
\textup{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{ur}}/K)
. Denote by
I\vcentcolon=\ker(\textup{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)\to\Gamma)
the inertia group of
K
.

Remark 2.1
.

As
K^{\mathrm{ur}}
is another discretely valued field with perfect residue field, we will often apply to
K^{\mathrm{ur}}
without comment definitions which are initially stated for
K
.

2.1.1.
The building and its functorialities

Assume
G
is a reductive group over
K
. As in
[
27
, Definition 7.6.1]
(cf.
[
27
, Definition 9.2.8]
), associated to
G
is a building (in the sense of
[
27
, Definition 1.5.5]
)

\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\vcentcolon=\mathscr{B}(G_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}},{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}),

where the right-hand side is as in
[
27
, Definition 7.6.1]
. This building comes equipped with an action of
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\rtimes\Gamma
. Moreover, the action of
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
factors through the natural map
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to G^{\textup{ad}}({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
.

Additionally, we have a building

\mathscr{B}(G,K)\vcentcolon=\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\Gamma},

which naturally comes equipped with an action of
G(K)
which factorizes through the map
G(K)\to G^{\textup{ad}}(K)
. To distinguish these two objects, we call the
\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
the
building associated to
G
over
{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}
and
\mathscr{B}(G,K)
the
building associated to
G
over
K
.

Remark 2.2
.

The building(s) we are using here are more often called the
reduced Bruhat–Tits building(s)
. While there exists a notion of the
extended Bruhat–Tits building(s)
, we will not need these notions here and, in fact, this simpler object is better suited to our purposes. See
[
27
]
for more details.

Various functorial properties for these buildings have been established in the literature, see
[
30
]
and
[
27
, §14]
. However, in this article we require only two basic functoriality statements.

The first concerns quotient maps of reductive groups.

Lemma 2.3

(cf.
[
27
, Proposition 14.1.1]
)
.

Suppose that
f\colon G\to H
is a faithfully flat map of reductive groups over
K
. Then, there exists a unique surjective map

f_{\ast}\colon\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\mathscr{B}(H,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}),

which is equivariant for the map
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\rtimes\Gamma\to H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\rtimes\Gamma
. A similar statement holds for the buildings over
K
.

For the second statement, we first make the following definition. For a field
F
, a map
f\colon G\to H
of reductive groups over
F
is an
ad-isomorphism
if
f(Z(G))\subseteq Z(H)
, and the induced map
f^{\textup{ad}}\colon G^{\textup{ad}}\to H^{\textup{ad}}
is an isomorphism. We record for later use the following proposition, whose proof is an exercise in assembling well-known facts about reductive groups over fields (e.g., see
[
37
]
).

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
f\colon G\to H
be a map of reductive groups over
F
. Then, the following conditions on
f
are equivalent:

(1)

f
is an ad-isomorphism,

(2)

f^{-1}(Z(H))=Z(G)
,

(3)

the induced map
f\colon G^{\textup{der}}\to H^{\textup{der}}
is a central isogeny.
2
2

2

Any isogeny is central if it is of degree coprime to the characteristic of
F
(see
[
37
, Remark 12.39]
).

The second basic functoriality result is the following.

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose
f\colon G\to H
is an ad-isomorphism of reductive groups over
K
. Then
f
induces a bijection of buildings

\displaystyle f_{\ast}\colon\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.68887pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathscr{B}(H,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}),

equivariant for the map
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\rtimes\Gamma\to H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\rtimes\Gamma
. Moreover, if
g\colon H\to L
is another ad-isomorphism, then
(g\circ f)_{\ast}=g_{\ast}\circ f_{\ast}
. A similar statement holds for buildings over
K
.

Proof.

As discussed on
[
27
, p. 264]
, there are natural
\Gamma
-equivariant bijections

\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathscr{B}(G^{\textup{ad}},{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}),\qquad\mathscr{B}(H,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathscr{B}(H^{\textup{ad}},{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}),

which are equivariant for the maps
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to G^{\textup{ad}}({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
and
H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to H^{\textup{ad}}({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
, respectively. These, together with the
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection
\mathscr{B}(G^{\textup{ad}},{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathscr{B}(H^{\textup{ad}},{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
induced by
f^{\textup{ad}}
, gives us a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection
\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\mathscr{B}(H,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
. That this is equivariant for the map
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
is clear from construction as
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
and
H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
act through their images in
G^{\textup{ad}}({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
and
H^{\textup{ad}}({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
, respectively. The proof for the buildings over
K
follows by passing to the fixed points for
\Gamma
.
∎

2.1.2.
Parahoric groups and group schemes

Let
G
be a reductive group over
K
. As in
[
25
, §7]
(see also
[
27
, §11.1]
), one may construct a group homomorphism

\kappa_{G}\colon G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\pi_{1}(G)_{I},

where
\pi_{1}(G)
denotes the fundamental group of Borovoi (see
[
4
, §1]
), a finitely generated abelian group, and the subscript
(-)_{I}
denotes
I
-coinvariants. We call
\kappa_{G}
the
Kottwitz homomorphism
associated to
G
. We let
\kappa_{G}\otimes 1
denote the composition of
\kappa_{G}
with the map
\pi_{1}(G)_{I}\to\pi_{1}(G)_{I}\otimes{Z}\mathbb{Q}
.

We denote the kernel of
\kappa_{G}
and
\kappa_{G}\otimes 1
by
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{0}
and
G(K^{\mathrm{ur}})^{1}
, respectively.
3
3

3

The groups
G(K^{\mathrm{ur}})^{0}
and
G(K^{\mathrm{ur}})^{1}
may be given more concrete definitions. See
[
27
, Notation 2.6.15 and Definition 2.6.23]
for these definitions, and
[
27
, Lemma 11.5.2 and Proposition 11.5.4]
for the proofs that these groups coincides with our definition.
The Kottwitz homomorphism (and thus the subgroups
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{0}
and
G(K^{\mathrm{ur}})^{1}
) is functorial in
G
. More precisely, if
f\colon G\to H
is a map of reductive groups over
K
, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{1}(G)_{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{1}(H)_{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(f)}

commutes.

We then have the following definition of a parahoric group scheme.

Definition 2.6
.

A group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme
\mathcal{G}
is
parahoric
if:

(1)

G\vcentcolon=\mathcal{G}_{K}
is a reductive group over
K
,

(2)

\mathcal{G}
is a smooth affine group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme with connected fibers,

(3)

there exists a point
x
in
\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
(equiv.
\mathscr{B}(G,K)
) such that

(2.1)

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})=G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{0}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}(x).

Remark 2.7
.

That this definition agrees with that originally defined in
[
6
]
is (essentially) the content of
[
20
, Proposition 3]
.

As in
[
27
, §9.2.6]
, associated to any point
x
of
\mathscr{B}(G,K)
is a parahoric group scheme denoted by
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
, characterized by Equation (
2.1
). Moreover, every parahoric group scheme arises in this way. A subgroup of
G(K)
is called
parahoric
if it is of the form
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
for a parahoric group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme
\mathcal{G}
.

2.1.3.
Parahoric models of tori and
R
-smooth tori

Suppose that
T
is a a torus over
K
. By Lemma
2.5
it’s clear that
\mathscr{B}(T,K)
is a singleton, and thus that
T
has a unique parahoric model which we denote by
\mathcal{T}
. As
\mathscr{B}(T,K)
is a singleton,
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
is equal to
T(K)^{0}
.

Let
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{lft}}
be the Néron model of
T
(e.g., see
[
27
, §B.7–B.8]
and
[
2
]
).

Proposition 2.8

(see
[
27
, §B.7–B.8]
)
.

There is a functorial identification
\mathcal{T}\simeq(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{lft}})^{\circ}
.

Fix a finite Galois extension
K_{1}
of
K
splitting
T
, and set
T_{1}=\mathrm{Res}_{K_{1}/K}T_{K_{1}}
which admits a natural embedding
T\to T_{1}
.

Definition 2.9

(
[
29
, Definition 2.4.3]
)
.

The torus
T
is called
R
-smooth
if its Zariski closure in
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\mathrm{lft}}
is a smooth group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme.
4
4

4

As in
[
6
, §4.4.8]
, one may show that the definition of being
R
-smooth does not depend on the choice of
K_{1}
.

Consider now a reductive group
G
over
K
. As
G_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}
is quasi-split by Steinberg’s theorem (see
[
48
, Theorem 1.9]
), there is a unique
G(K^{\mathrm{ur}})
-conjugacy class of maximally split maximal tori in
G_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}
which are precisely the centralizers of maximal split tori (see
[
3
, §20]
).

Definition 2.10
.

We say that a reductive
K
-group
G
is
R
-smooth
if one (equivalently any) maximally split maximal torus of
G_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}
is
R
-smooth.

As the formation of Néron models commutes with unramified base change, Definition
2.10
agrees with that of Definition
2.9
when
G
is a torus.

2.1.4.
Abelianization of parahoric group schemes

Suppose that
G
is a reductive group over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, and consider the short exact sequence of reductive groups

1\to G^{\textup{der}}\xrightarrow{i}G\xrightarrow{f}G^{\textup{ab}}\to 1.

By Lemma
2.3
and Lemma
2.5
we obtain maps of buildings

i_{\ast}\colon\mathscr{B}(G^{\textup{der}},K)\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathscr{B}(G,K),\qquad f_{\ast}\colon\mathscr{B}(G,K)\to\mathscr{B}(G^{\textup{ab}},K),

where the first map is a bijection and the second is surjective, and the maps are equivariant for
G^{\textup{der}}(K)\to G(K)
and
G(K)\to G^{\textup{ab}}(K)
, respectively. Similar statements hold for the buildings over
{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}
.

Let us fix a point
y
of
\mathscr{B}(G,K)
and set
x=i_{\ast}^{-1}(y)
and
z=f_{\ast}(z)
. By the functoriality of the Kottwitz homomorphism and the equivariant properties of these maps we see by
[
27
, Corollary 2.10.10]
that
i
and
f
extend to maps
i\colon\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}
and
f\colon\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}
.

Proposition 2.11
.

If
G^{\textup{der}}
is
R
-smooth, then we have a short exact sequence

1\to\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}\to 1,

of group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-schemes.

Proof.

First observe that by the setup, we have a diagram

\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}.

We first claim that
\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
is surjective. Let
T
be a maximally split maximal torus in
G_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}
as in
[
27
, §8.3.6]
. Then as in loc. cit. there exists a map
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
, thus it suffices to show that
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
is surjective. That said, observe that
\ker(T\to G^{\textup{ab}})=T\cap G^{\textup{der}}
is a torus and therefore, by
[
27
, Lemma 2.5.20]
the map
T(K^{\mathrm{ur}})^{0}\to G^{\textup{ab}}(K^{\mathrm{ur}})^{0}
is surjective, so the claim follows.

We now prove that
f\colon\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}
is faithfully flat. To see that
f
is surjective, we observe that
f
is surjective over
K
, being the map
G\to G^{\textup{ab}}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
f_{k^{\mathrm{sep}}}
contains every point of
\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}(k^{\mathrm{sep}})
in its image. As
\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}
is smooth, we know that
\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}(k^{\mathrm{sep}})
is surjective (e.g., see
[
16
, Thèoréme 18.5.17]
), and, since
\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
is surjective, the claim follows. To show that
f
is flat, we may, by the critére de platitude par fibres (see
[
15
, Thèorḿe 11.3.10]
), show instead that
f_{K}
and
f_{k}
are flat. But
\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ}\to\textup{Spec}\,(\mathcal{O}_{K})
is smooth (and so
(\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ})_{K}
and
(\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ})_{k}
are reduced), so this follows from
[
37
, Theorem 3.34]
.

Finally, since
G^{\textup{der}}
is
R
-smooth, it follows from
[
29
, Proposition 2.4.9]
that the map
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}
is a closed immersion. Moreover, as
\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}^{\circ}_{z}
is flat, the group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme
\ker(\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ})
is flat. So, to show that the closed immersion
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}
identifies
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
with
\ker(\mathcal{G}_{y}^{\circ}\to\mathcal{G}_{z}^{\circ})
it suffices to check this claim on the generic fiber,
5
5

5

Suppose that
X
is a flat
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme and
Z_{i}\subseteq X
are
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-flat closed subschemes such that
(Z_{1})_{K}=(Z_{2})_{K}
, then
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
. Without loss of generality,
X=\textup{Spec}\,(R)
for some
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-algebra
R
. Write
I_{i}
for the ideal defining
Z_{i}
. Let
x
belong to
I_{1}
. Then
(Z_{1})_{K}=(Z_{2})_{K}
, so
x
belongs to
(I_{2})_{K}
and thus there exists some
m\geqslant 0
such that
\pi^{m}x
belongs to
I_{2}
, with
\pi
a uniformizer of
K
. So,
\pi^{m}x
is zero in
R/I_{2}
which, as
R/I_{2}
is
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-flat, implies that
x
is zero in
R/I_{2}
, and so belongs to
I_{2}
. So,
I_{1}\subseteq I_{2}
. By symmetry,
I_{2}\subseteq I_{1}
.
where it is clear.
∎

In practice, if we have fixed a parahoric model
\mathcal{G}
of
G
, then we write the unique parahoric model of
G^{\textup{ab}}
by
\mathcal{G}^{\textup{ab}}
. Given Proposition
2.11
this notational abuse is not too severe. Similarly, we denote the associated parahoric model of
G^{\mathrm{der}}
by
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{der}}
.

2.1.5.
Parahoric group schemes and central quotients

Let
f\colon G\to G^{\prime}
be a faithfully flat map such that
Z\vcentcolon=\ker(f)
is a torus over
K
. Let
x
be a point of
\mathscr{B}(G,K)
and
x^{\prime}
its image under the map the map
f_{\ast}
from Lemma
2.3
. Let us write
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}_{x^{\prime}}^{\circ}

As
f_{\ast}
is equivariant for the map
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to G^{\prime}({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
and the Kottwitz homomorphism is functorial, we deduce that
f
maps
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
into
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
. Thus, by
[
27
, Corollary 2.10.10]

f
uniquely lifts to a map
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. When
Z
is
R
-smooth, one can say more.

Proposition 2.12

(
[
29
, Proposition 2.4.14]
)
.

Suppose that
Z
is an
R
-smooth torus. Then, there exists a short exact sequence of group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-schemes

1\to\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to 1,

where
\mathcal{Z}
is the Zariski closure of
Z
in
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{Z}
is a smooth group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme.

2.1.6.
Parahoric group schemes and ad-isomorphisms

Suppose that
i\colon G\to H
is an injective ad-isomorphism of reductive groups over
K
. Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
is a parahoric model of
H
, and
y
is a point of
\mathscr{B}(H,k)
such that
\mathcal{H}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{y}^{\circ}
. By Lemma
2.5
there is a unique point
x
of
\mathscr{B}(G,K)
such that
y=i_{\ast}(x)
.

The following basic proposition provides a recognition principle for
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
.

Proposition 2.13
.

A model
\mathcal{G}
of
G
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
if it is a smooth affine group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme with connected fibers and

(2.2)

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})=\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\cap G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}}).

Moreover,
i
extends to a map of
\mathcal{O}_{k}
-group schemes
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
which is a closed embedding if
G
is
R
-smooth.

Proof.

By the definition of parahoric group schemes, and the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
, it suffices to show that

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})=G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{0}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}(x).

Since
i_{\ast}
defines a bijection
\mathscr{B}(G,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathscr{B}(H,{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})

which is equivariant for the map
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\to H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
, we deduce that

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Stab}_{G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}(x)=\mathrm{Stab}_{H({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}(y)\cap G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}}).

The inclusion
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}_{G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}(x)
then follows immediately from (
2.2
). Additionally, as
\mathcal{G}
is a smooth affine group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme with connected fibers, it follows from
[
27
, Proposition 8.3.15]
that
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\subseteq G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{0}
. Thus,
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\subseteq G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{0}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}(x)
.

On the other hand, if
g
belongs to
G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})^{0}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{G({K^{\mathrm{ur}}})}(x)
, then
i(g)
belongs to
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
by functoriality of the Kottwitz homomorphism along with the identity (
2.4
). Hence (
2.2
) implies that
g
belongs to
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})
.

Finally, the fact that
i
extends to a morphism
i\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
follows from
[
27
, Corollary 2.10.10]
by (
2.2
). That
i
is a closed embedding if
G
is
R
-smooth follows from
[
29
, Proposition 2.4.9]
, whose proof works under the assumption that
i
is an ad-isomorphism.
∎

2.2.
Fiber products of groups

In this subsection we aim to collect some facts concerning fiber products of groups, in various contexts, that will be useful below.

2.2.1.
Smoothness and connectedness of fiber products of group schemes

Fix a connected scheme
S
and consider a Cartesian diagram of group
S
-schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Set
\mathcal{K}
to be the group
S
-scheme
\ker(f)
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Suppose that
f
is faithfully flat and quasi-compact, and that
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{C}
are smooth group
S
-schemes with connected fibers. Then,
\mathcal{D}
is a smooth group
S
-scheme with connected fibers.

One may replace all instances of “connected” in the assumptions and conclusion of the above statement by “geometrically connected”, “irreducible”, or “geometrically irreducible” by standard theory concerning algebraic groups (see
[
37
, Summary 1.36]
). We use this observation freely below.

Proof of Proposition
2.14
.

The morphism
\mathcal{A}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{K}\times_{S}\mathcal{A}
given by
(g,h)\mapsto(gh^{-1},h)
is an isomorphism. But, as
\mathcal{K}\to S
is smooth, thus so is
\mathcal{K}\times_{S}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
, and thus so is
\mathcal{A}\times_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
. As
f
is faitfully flat and quasi-compact, we deduce by fpqc descent for smoothness (e.g., see
[
47
,
Tag 0429
]
) that
f
is smooth. By stability of smoothness under base change we deduce that
q\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
is smooth, and as
\mathcal{C}\to S
is smooth thus so is the composition
\mathcal{D}\to S
.

To prove that
\mathcal{D}\to S
has connected fibers, let
s
be an element of
S
and consider the map
q_{s}\colon\mathcal{D}_{s}\to\mathcal{C}_{s}
. By assumption we have that
\mathcal{C}_{s}
is connected and
q_{s}\colon\mathcal{D}_{s}\to\mathcal{C}_{s}
is surjective. Moreover, by the argument in the previous paragraph we have that
q_{s}\colon\mathcal{D}_{s}\to\mathcal{C}_{s}
is smooth, and thus open. On the other hand, as
\mathcal{K}\to S
has geometrically connected fibers, the same holds true for
f
as these fibers are translations of geometric fibers of
\mathcal{K}\to S
. Thus, the map
q_{s}
has geometrically connected fibers (see
[
47
,
Tag 055E
]
). Therefore we deduce that
\mathcal{D}_{s}
is connected as desired from the simple fact that if
f\colon Y\to X
be an open surjective map of topological spaces with connected fibers and
X
is connected, then
Y
is connected.
∎

2.2.2.
Some fiber products of parahoric group schemes

Suppose now that, as in §
2.1
,
K
is a discretely valued field with perfect residue field. Suppose further that we have a Cartesian diagram of group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{C}
are parahoric group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-schemes. For simplicity we denote the generic fiber of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{D}
, by
A,B,C,
and
D
, respectively.

Proposition 2.15
.

Suppose that
f
is faithfully flat and quasi-compact, both
B
and
C
are tori, and
\ker(f)
is a smooth group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme with connected fibers. Then,
\mathcal{D}
is a parahoric group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
, then
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{G}^{\circ}_{(x,\ast)}
under the isomorphism

\mathscr{B}(D,K)\to\mathscr{B}(A\times_{\textup{Spec}\,(K)}C,K)\simeq\mathscr{B}(A,K)\times\{\ast\}

from Proposition
2.5
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.14
, we know that
\mathcal{D}
is a smooth group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme with connected fibers. Moreover, observe that we have a natural short exact sequence of group
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-schemes

1\to\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}\times_{\textup{Spec}\,(\mathcal{O}_{K})}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{B}\to 1,

where the map
\mathcal{A}\times_{\textup{Spec}\,(\mathcal{O}_{K})}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{B}
is given by sending
(a,c)
to
f(a)g(c)^{-1}
. This implies that
D
is a normal subgroup of the reductive group
A\times_{\textup{Spec}\,(K)}C
and so reductive (see
[
37
, Corollary 21.53]
).

On the other hand, this exact sequence also implies that
{D}\to{A}\times_{\textup{Spec}\,(K)}{C}
induces an isomorphism on adjoint subgroups as
{B}
is abelian. Moreover, observe that evidently

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})=(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}})\times\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathrm{ur}}}))\cap D(K^{\mathrm{ur}}).

The claim then follows directly from Proposition
2.13
.
∎

2.2.3.
Torsors for fiber products of groups

We next are interested in understanding the relationship between torsors for a fiber product of groups, and torsors for their constituent groups. The correct generality for this discussion is that of topoi. We follow the notation and conventions concerning torsors and topoi as found in
[
21
, §A.1]
.

Let us fix a topos
\mathscr{T}
with final object
\ast
, and a Cartesian diagram of group objects

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Consider the
2
-fiber product of groupoids

\mathbf{Tors}(\mathcal{A})\times_{f_{\ast},\mathbf{Tors}(\mathcal{B}),g_{\ast}}\mathbf{Tors}(\mathcal{C}),

(e.g., see
[
47
,
Tag 02X9
]
). Observe that there is a natural equivalence of functors

\theta_{\mathrm{nat}}\colon f_{\ast}\circ p_{\ast}\simeq(f\circ p)_{\ast}=(g\circ q)_{\ast}\simeq g_{\ast}\circ q_{\ast},

and thus the triple
(p_{\ast},q_{\ast},\theta_{\mathrm{nat}})
determines an object of the
2
-fiber product. Thus,

(2.5)

\Psi\colon\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathscr{T})\to\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathscr{T})\times_{f_{\ast},\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathscr{T}),g_{\ast}}\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{T}),

given by
\Psi(\mathcal{P})=(p_{\ast}\mathcal{P},q_{\ast}\mathcal{P},\theta_{\mathrm{nat}})
is a morphism of groupoids.

Conversely, suppose that
(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}},\theta)
is an object of
\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathscr{T})\times_{f_{\ast},\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathscr{T}),g_{\ast}}\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{T})
. Let us observe that we have a natural map

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\to f_{\ast}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}})/\mathcal{A},

induced by the map
(e,\mathrm{id})\colon\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}
, where
e
denotes the identity section of
\mathcal{B}
. We similarly have a map
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}\to g_{\ast}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Let us define

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}\vcentcolon=\lim\left(\hbox to166.28pt{\vbox to50.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 83.14063pt\lower-25.15279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where this limit is taken in
\mathscr{T}
. It is simple to check that there is a unique
\mathcal{D}
-action on
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
for which the natural map
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
is equivariant for the map
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}
. This
\mathcal{D}
-object of
\mathscr{T}
is evidently functorial in the triple
(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}},\theta)
.

Note that it is not a priori clear that
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a
\mathcal{D}
-torsor. That said, we do have the following affirmative result in this direction under the assumption that
f
is an epimorphism.

Proposition 2.16
.

Suppose that
f
is an epimorphism. Then, the functor

\Phi\colon\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathscr{T})\times_{f_{\ast},\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathscr{T}),g_{\ast}}\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{T})\to\mathbf{Tors}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathscr{T})

given by
\Phi(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}},\theta)=\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
, is a well-defined quasi-inverse to
\Psi
. Moreover, the equivalences
\Psi
and
\Phi
are compatible with pullbacks along a morphism of topoi
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{T}
.

Proof.

To show that
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a
\mathcal{D}
-torsor we are free to localize
\mathscr{T}
. In particular, we may assume that
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}
is trivializable. Choose a trivialization corresponding to an element
x
of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}(\ast)
. Consider then the induced element of
g_{\ast}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}(\ast)
, which we also denote by
x
, and the resulting element
\theta^{-1}(x)
of
f_{\ast}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}(\ast)
. As
f
is an epimorphism we may, after possibly localizing
\mathscr{T}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{21.52098pt}{-22.35971pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

As
\mathcal{A}\times_{\mathrm{id}}\mathcal{C}
is evidently the trivial
\mathcal{D}
-torsor, the claim follows.

To prove that
\Phi\circ\Psi
is naturally isomorphic to the identity, it suffices to prove the following claim: for a
\mathcal{D}
-torsor
\mathcal{P}
the natural map

\mathcal{P}\to f_{\ast}\mathcal{P}\times g_{\ast}\mathcal{P},

is equivariant for the map
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}
, and factorizes uniquely through a
\mathcal{D}
-equivariant map
\mathcal{P}\to f_{\ast}\mathcal{P}\times_{\theta}g_{\ast}\mathcal{P}
. But this claim can be checked after localizing on
\mathscr{T}
, which reduces us to the trivial case as in the previous paragraph.

To prove that
\Psi\circ\Phi
is naturally isomorphic to the identity, let us fix an object
(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}},\theta)
of the
2
-fiber product. Let us observe that we have a natural projection map

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}

which is equivariant for the map
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}
. By the adjunction in
[
12
, Chapitre III, Proposition 1.3.6 (iii)]
we obtain a unique
\mathcal{A}
-equivariant map

\alpha\colon f_{\ast}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}})\to\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}},

which is necessarily an isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}
-torsors. Similarly, we obtain an isomorphism

\beta\colon g_{\ast}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}}\times_{\theta}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}})\to\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}}.

It is then easy to check that
(\alpha,\beta)
defines a natural morphism

\Psi(\Phi(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}},\theta))\to(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{C}},\theta),

where the matching of
\theta_{\mathrm{nat}}
in the source and
\theta
in the target may be checked locally on
\mathscr{T}
, from where we may reduce to the trivial case where it is clear. As the
2
-fiber product is a groupoid, we deduce that
(\alpha,\beta)
is a natural isomorphism.

The final compatibility claim is clear by construction.
∎

2.2.4.
Shtukas for fiber products of groups

We finally record the natural implication of Proposition
2.16
for shtukas over a pre-adic space in the sense of
[
42
, §2.3]
.

To this end, let us fix a fiber product of group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{C}
are smooth group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-schemes with connected fibers,
f
is faithfully flat and quasi-compact, and
\ker(f)
is a smooth group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-scheme with connected fibers. We then see by Proposition
2.14
that
\mathcal{D}
is a smooth group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-scheme with connected fibers. Further fix a discretely valued algebraic extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with residue field
k_{E}
,
and fix a pre-adic space
\mathscr{X}
over
\mathcal{O}_{E}
. We then have the following result.

Corollary 2.17
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\mathcal{D}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(\mathscr{X})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.68887pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{A}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(\mathscr{X})\times_{f_{\ast},\mathcal{B}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(\mathscr{X}),g_{\ast}}\mathcal{C}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(\mathscr{X}).

Proof.

Fix an morphism
\alpha\colon S\to\mathscr{X}^{\lozenge/}
, where
S
is an object of
\mathbf{Perf}_{k_{E}}
, with corresponding untilt
S^{\sharp}
over
\mathcal{O}_{E}
. Then, it immediately by applying Proposition
2.16
to the étale topoi of
S\dot{\times}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
S\dot{\times}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\setminus S^{\sharp}
, that there are equivalences

\mathcal{D}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(S)\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{A}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(S)\times_{f_{\ast},\mathcal{B}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(S),g_{\ast}}\mathcal{C}\text{-}\mathbf{Sht}(S).

As this equivalence is
2
-functorial in the topos, the claim then follows by letting
S
vary.
∎

3.
Integral models of Shimura varieties at parahoric level

In this section we study the Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models of Shimura varieties of abelian type at parahoric level as constructed in
[
26
]
and
[
29
]
.

3.1.
Existence of models and their properties

In this subsection we precisely state the existence of integral models at parahoric level and list some of their properties which are most relevant for our purposes.

3.1.1.
Shimura varieties

We begin by recalling some definitions and notation from the theory of Shimura varieties. Let
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
be a Shimura datum, meaning that
{\mathbf{G}}
is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
, and
\mathbf{X}
is a
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of homomorphisms

\displaystyle h:\mathbb{S}\to{\mathbf{G}}{R},

(where
\mathbb{S}={\mathrm{Res}}_{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}}\,\mathbb{G}_{m/\mathbb{C}}
is the Deligne torus), satisfying the axioms (SV1)-(SV3) in
[
35
, Definition 5.5]
. Associated to
\mathbf{X}
is a unique conjugacy class
\mbox{$\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt$}{}_{h}
of coharacters
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbf{G}{C}
(see
[
35
, p. 344]
). We denote the field of definition of
\mbox{$\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt$}{}_{h}
by
{\mathbf{E}}
, and call it the reflex field of
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
(see
[
35
, Definition 12.2]
).

We often fix a place
v
of
\mathbf{E}
dividing
p
, and write
E
for the completion
\mathbf{E}_{v}
. Using
[
23
, Lemma 1.1.3]
,
\mbox{$\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt$}{}_{h}
gives rise to a unique conjugacy class of cocharacters
\mathbb{G}_{m,\overline{E}}\to G_{\overline{E}}
, which we also denote by
\mbox{$\raisebox{-2.54025pt}{$l$}\hskip-1.79993pt\mu\hskip-8.80005pt\raisebox{-4.2194pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}}\hskip-4.16pt\raisebox{3.7889pt}{$\color[rgb]{1,1,1}.$}\hskip 4.60007pt$}{}_{h}
. One may check that this conjugacy class has field of definition
E
, and so we call this the local reflex field.

For any neat compact open subgroup
{\sf K}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
(see
[
35
, p. 288]
), we attach to
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
and
{\sf K}
the Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
, which is a quasi-projective variety over
\mathbb{C}
, whose
\mathbb{C}
-points are given by the equality

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})(\mathbb{C})={\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathbf{X}\times{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/{\sf K}.

We write an element of this double quotient as
[x,g]_{\sf K}
. As in
[
8
]
(cf.
[
38
]
),
\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
admits a canonical model over the reflex field
{\mathbf{E}}
. Hereafter we will use
\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
to denote the canonical model over
{\mathbf{E}}
.

For a containment
{\sf K}\subseteq{\sf K}^{\prime}
of neat compact open subgroups of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and
g
in
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
such that
g^{-1}{\sf K}g\subseteq{\sf K}^{\prime}
, there is a unique finite étale morphism of
{\mathbf{E}}
-schemes

\displaystyle t_{{\sf K},{\sf K}^{\prime}}(g):\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\to\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X}),

which is given on
\mathbb{C}
-points by the identity

\displaystyle t_{{\sf K},{\sf K}^{\prime}}(g)[x,g^{\prime}]_{\sf K}=[x,g^{\prime}g]_{{\sf K}^{\prime}}.

We write
\pi_{{\sf K},{\sf K}^{\prime}}
for
t_{{\sf K},{\sf K}^{\prime}}(\textup{id})
, and
[g]_{\sf K}
for
t_{{\sf K},g^{-1}{\sf K}g}(g)
.

The morphisms
\pi_{{\sf K},{\sf K}^{\prime}}
form a projective system
\{\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\}_{\sf K}
with finite étale transition morphisms. We define

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\vcentcolon=\varprojlim_{\sf K}\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X}),

where the limit is taken over all neat compact open subgroups
{\sf K}
of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Since each
\pi_{{\sf K},{\sf K}^{\prime}}
is affine, the limit in (
3.1
) exists as an
{\mathbf{E}}
-scheme (see
[
47
,
Tag 01YX
]
). The morphisms
[g]_{\sf K}
endow
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
with a continuous action of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
in the sense of
[
8
, 2.7.1]
.

We will often fix a prime
p
and consider neat compact open subgroups
{\sf K}={\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}
with
{\sf K}_{p}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
{\sf K}^{p}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
. We define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\vcentcolon=\varprojlim_{{\sf K}^{p}}\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}{\sf K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X}),

where
{\sf K}^{p}
varies over neat compact open subgroups of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
. As in the case of
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
, we see that
\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}_{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is a scheme over
{\mathbf{E}}
with a continuous action of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
.

Remark 3.1
.

When several Shimura data are simultaneously under consideration, we will differentiate them with numerical subscripts (e.g.,
(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})
) and use the same numerical subscripts to denote the objects defined above (or below) for this Shimura datum (e.g.,
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}_{p,1}\mathsf{K}^{p}_{1}}(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})
).

A morphism of Shimura data
\alpha\colon(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})\to(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is a morphism of group
\mathbb{Q}
-schemes
\alpha\colon\mathbf{G}_{1}\to\mathbf{G}
with the property that
\alpha{R}\circ h_{1}
belongs to
\mathbf{X}
for
h_{1}
in
\mathbf{X}_{1}
. We say that
\alpha
is an embedding if
\mathbf{G}_{1}\to\mathbf{G}
is a closed embedding. From
[
8
, §5]
, for a morphism of Shimura data
\alpha
, one has that
\mathbf{E}\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{1}
and there is a unique morphism
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})\to\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{\mathbf{E}_{1}}
of
\mathbf{E}_{1}
-schemes equivariant for the map
\alpha\colon\mathbf{G}_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\to\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and such that if
\alpha(\mathsf{K}_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{K}
then the induced map on the quotients
\alpha_{\mathsf{K}_{1},\mathsf{K}}\colon\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{E_{1}}
is given on
\mathbb{C}
-points by

\alpha_{\mathsf{K}_{1},\mathsf{K}}\left([x_{1},g_{1}]_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}\right)=[\alpha\circ x_{1},\alpha(g_{1})]_{\mathsf{K}}.

We say that
\alpha
is an
ad-isomorphism
if
\alpha\colon\mathbf{G}_{1}\to\mathbf{G}
is an ad-isomorphism and moreover, that
\alpha\colon(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{\mathrm{ad}},\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\mathrm{ad}})\to(\mathbf{G}^{\mathrm{ad}},\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{ad}})
is an isomorphism.

3.1.2.
Parahoric Shimura data

We say that a triple
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
is a
parahoric Shimura datum
if
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is a Shimura datum, and
\mathcal{G}
is a parahoric model of
G\vcentcolon=\mathbf{G}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. By a morphism
\alpha\colon(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{1})\to(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
of parahoric Shimura data we mean a morphism
\alpha\colon(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})\to(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
of Shimura data together with a specified model
\mathcal{G}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}
of
G_{1}\to G
, which we also denote
\alpha
. By
[
27
, Corollary 2.10.10]
, such an
\alpha
is unique, and it exists if and only if
\alpha\colon G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\to G_{1}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
maps
\mathcal{G}_{1}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
into
\mathcal{G}(\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
. We say that
\alpha
is an embedding or an ad-isomorphism if
\alpha\colon(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})\to(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is.

Given a parahoric Shimura datum
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
we often use the following shorthand

\mathsf{K}_{p}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Choosing a point
x
of
\mathscr{B}(G,\mathbb{Q}_{p})
such that
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
, we write

\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{p}\vcentcolon=G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}})^{1}\cap\mathrm{Stab}_{G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(x),

which is a a supergroup of
\mathsf{K}_{p}
of finite index.

The following lemma will be important for later constructions. Let us denote by
\mathbf{E}^{p}
the maximal extension of
\mathbf{E}
unramified at places dividing
p
.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
26
, Corollary 4.3.9]
)
.

The natural map

\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{\overline{\mathbf{E}}})\to\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{\mathbf{E}^{p}})

is a bijection.

In other words, this result says that the action of
\textup{Gal}(\overline{\mathbf{E}}/\mathbf{E})
on
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{\overline{\mathbf{E}}})
factorizes through
\textup{Gal}(\mathbf{E}^{p}/\mathbf{E})
(cf.
[
47
,
Tag 038D
]
).

3.1.3.
Some conditions on a Shimura datum

The construction of Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models involves several conditions on a Shimura datum that we presently recall.

We start with the following standard definitions, in increasing level of generality.

Definition 3.3
.

We say that
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is

•

of
Siegel type
if it is of the form
(\mathrm{GSp}(\mathbf{V}),\mathfrak{h}^{\pm})
where
\mathbf{V}
is a symplectic
\mathbb{Q}
-space, and
\mathfrak{h}^{\pm}
is the union of the upper and lower Siegel half-spaces (e.g., see
[
35
, §6]
),

•

of
Hodge type
if there exists an embedding of Shimura data
\iota\colon(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\to(\mathrm{GSp}(\mathbf{V}),\mathfrak{h}^{\pm})
(called a
Hodge embedding
),

•

of
abelian type
if there exists a Shimura datum
(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})
of Hodge type and an isogeny
\alpha\colon\mathbf{G}_{1}^{\textup{der}}\to\mathbf{G}^{\textup{der}}
inducing an isomorphism
(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{\textup{ad}},\mathbf{X}^{\textup{ad}})\to(\mathbf{G}^{\textup{ad}},\mathbf{X}^{\textup{ad}})
, in which case we say that
(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})
is
adapted
to
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
, leaving the
\alpha
implicit.

We have the following examples of Shimura data of abelian type.
6
6

6

An essentially full classification of Shimura varieties of abelian type is given in the appendix of
[
40
]
.

Example 3.4
.

Shimura data of PEL type (see
[
35
, §8]
) are of Hodge type.

Example 3.5
.

Let
\mathbf{V}
be a quadratic space over
\mathbb{Q}
of signature
(n,2)
. The group
\mathrm{GSpin}(\mathbf{V})
acts transitively on the space
\mathbf{X}
of oriented negative definite
2
-planes in
\mathbf{V}{R}
, and
\mathbf{X}
can be identified with a
\mathrm{GSpin}(\mathbf{V})(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of morphisms
\mathbb{S}\to\mathrm{GSpin}(\mathbf{V}){R}
(see
[
39
, §1]
). The pair
(\mathrm{GSpin}(\mathbf{V}),\mathbf{X})
is a Shimura datum of Hodge type which is not of PEL type (see
[
39
, §3]
).

Example 3.6
.

Let
F\supsetneq\mathbb{Q}
be a totally real field,
B
a quaternion algebra over
F
, and
\mathbf{G}_{B}
the algebraic
\mathbb{Q}
-group
B^{\times}
. There is a Shimura datum
(\mathbf{G}_{B},\mathbf{X}_{B})
associated to
B
(see
[
35
, Example 5.24]
). If
B
is not
\mathbb{R}
-split then
(\mathbf{G}_{B},\mathbf{X}_{B})
is of abelian type, but not of Hodge type. The Shimura varieties associated to such
(\mathbf{G}_{B},\mathbf{X}_{B})
include Shimura curves.

Example 3.7
.

For a
\mathbb{Q}
-torus
\mathbf{T}
any homomorphism
h\colon\mathbb{S}\to\mathbf{T}{R}
defines a Shimura datum
(\mathbf{T},\{h\})
of abelian type, which is rarely of Hodge type, and which we refer to as being of
toral type
.

Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models are constructed when
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is of abelian type and when the following group-theoretic property holds.

Definition 3.8

(
[
29
, Definition 3.3.2]
)
.

We say that
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is
acceptable
if
G^{\mathrm{ad}}
is isomorphic to a group of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{Res}_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\,H_{i}
, where each
F_{i}
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and
H_{i}
is an adjoint group over
F_{i}
which is split over a tame extension of
F_{i}
.

Remark 3.9
.

The acceptability condition is very mild. For instance, if
p>3
the condition is vacuous, and when
p=3
it only excludes those Shimura data
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X}
) for which
\mathbf{G}
has an almost simple factor of type
D_{4}
(see
[
29
, §3.3.1]
).

The construction of Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models will be an iterative process, starting with a much more restrictive class of Shimura data. For convenience, we give name to this class.

Definition 3.10
.

We say that
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is
very good
if:

•

(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is acceptable,

•

Z(\mathbf{G})
is a torus, and

•

Z(G)
,
G^{\textup{der}}
, and
G
are
R
-smooth (see Definitions
2.9
and
2.10
).

We say that a parahoric Shimura datum
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
is of Hodge type, abelian type, acceptable, or very good if the underlying Shimura datum
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is.

Definition 3.11
.

A parahoric Shimura datum
(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{1})
of Hodge type is
well-adapted
to
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
when given an isogeny
\alpha\colon\mathbf{G}_{1}^{\textup{der}}\to\mathbf{G}^{\textup{der}}
(often left implicit) such that

(1)

\alpha
adapts
(\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1})
to
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
,

(2)

if the isomorphism

\mathscr{B}(G_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{p})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathscr{B}(G,\mathbb{Q}_{p})

induced from the isogeny
\alpha\colon G_{1}^{\textup{der}}\to G^{\textup{der}}
and Lemma
2.5
has the property that if
\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
for
x
, and
x_{1}
denotes the corresponding point in
\mathscr{B}(G_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, then
\mathcal{G}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{G}_{x_{1}}^{\circ}
,

(3)

if
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{E}\mathbf{E}_{1}
, then
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
splits completely at every prime of
\mathbf{E}
lying over
p
.

Finally, we set some terminology used later on in the article. Let
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
be a parahoric Shimura datum of Hodge type. We call a map of Shimura data

\iota\colon(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})\to(\mathrm{GSp}(\mathbf{V}),\mathfrak{h}^{\pm},\mathrm{GSp}(\Lambda)),

where
\mathbf{V}
a symplectic
\mathbb{Q}
-space and
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbf{V}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is a self-dual
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattice, a
Hodge embedding of parahoric Shimura data
if the underling map
\iota\colon\mathbf{G}\to\mathrm{GSp}(\mathbf{V})
is a closed embedding. We say that
\iota

respects stabilizers
if
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{p}=\mathrm{GSp}(\Lambda)\cap\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

3.1.4.
Existence and properties of models

We are now prepared to state the existence of Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models, as well as the major properties we need of these models.

Theorem 3.12

(
[
26
, Theorem 4.6.23]
and
[
29
, Theorem 5.2.12]
)
.

Suppose that
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
is an acceptable parahoric Shimura datum of abelian type and. Then, there exists a system
\{\mathscr{S}^{\mathfrak{d}}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}\mathsf{K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\}
of normal flat quasi-projective
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-models of
\{\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}\mathsf{K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{E}\}
such that the following properties hold.

(1)

Fix neat compact open subgroups
\mathsf{K}^{p}
and
{\mathsf{K}^{p}}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
and an element
g
of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
with
g^{-1}{\sf K}^{p}g\subseteq{\mathsf{K}^{p}}^{\prime}
. Write
\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{K}_{p}\mathsf{K}^{p}
and
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}=\mathsf{K}_{p}{\mathsf{K}^{p}}^{\prime}
. Then, the morphism

t_{\mathsf{K},\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}(g):\mathrm{Sh}_{\sf K}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{E}\to\mathrm{Sh}_{{\sf K}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})_{E},

admits a (necessarily unique) finite étale extension

t_{\mathsf{K},\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}(g):\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathfrak{d}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\to\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{d}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X}).

(2)

Writing

\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}}^{\mathfrak{d}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})\vcentcolon=\varprojlim_{\mathsf{K}^{p}}\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}\mathsf{K}^{p}}^{\mathfrak{d}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X}),

one has the following
extension property
relative to a characteristic
(0,p)
discrete valuation ring
R
over
\mathcal{O}_{E}
: the natural map

\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}}^{\mathfrak{d}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})(R)\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})(R[\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}]),

is a bijection.

We will recall below, fairly precisely, the construction of the models
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathfrak{d}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
. The superscript
\mathfrak{d}
, which is either a very good parahoric Shimura datum of Hodge type well-adapted to
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
, or possibly
\varnothing
when
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X},\mathcal{G})
is of Hodge type. Its role is to emphasize various choices made in the construction of these models.

3.2.
The
\mathscr{A}
-group and the
\mathscr{E}
-group

Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be a reductive
\mathbb{Q}
-group. In this section we associate to
{\mathbf{G}}
groups
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})
and
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})^{\circ}
, and the so-called
\mathscr{E}
-group to a parahoric Shimura datum, following
[
8
]
. We also define analogs of these groups for
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-valued points following
[
26
]
and
[
29
]
, and discuss some functorial properties of these groups. These groups play an important role in the construction of the models
\mathscr{S}^{\mathfrak{d}}_{\mathsf{K}_{p}\mathsf{K}^{p}}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
from Theorem
3.12
.

3.2.1.
The
\ast
-product

Let us begin by recalling the
\ast
-product of Deligne (see
[
8
]
).

Suppose
H
and
\Gamma
are abstract groups equipped with actions of a group
\Delta
by group homomorphisms. Let

\displaystyle\psi:\Gamma\to H,\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi:\Gamma\to\Delta

be
\Delta
-equivariant group homomorphisms, where
\Delta
acts on itself by inner automorphisms, and assume that
\psi
and
\varphi
are compatible in the sense that, for all
\gamma
in
\Gamma
, we have the identity

\displaystyle\varphi(\gamma)\cdot h=\psi(\gamma)^{-1}h\psi(\gamma),

where
\cdot
denotes the action of
\Delta
on
H
. Then the semi-direct product
H\rtimes\Delta
admits a quotient

\displaystyle H\ast_{\Gamma}\Delta\vcentcolon=H\rtimes\Delta/\{(\psi(\gamma),\varphi(\gamma)^{-1})\},

where one easily checks that the subgroup
\{(\psi(\gamma),\varphi(\gamma)^{-1})\}
of all the elements of the form
(\psi(\gamma),\varphi(\gamma)^{-1})
for
\gamma
in
\Gamma
is a normal subgroup.

For the purposes of this section, we will refer to a datum
(H,\Gamma,\Delta,\psi,\varphi)
as above as a
tuple
. A morphism of tuples
(H,\Gamma,\Delta,\psi,\varphi)\to(H^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\psi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
is a triple
(f,g,h)
, consisting of group homomorphisms
f:H\to H^{\prime}
and
g:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
equivariant relative to a group homomorphism
h:\Delta\to\Delta^{\prime}
and such that
\psi^{\prime}\circ g=f\circ\psi
and
h\circ\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\circ f
.

The
\ast
-product satisfies the following elementary functoriality.

Lemma 3.13
.

Any morphism of tuples

\displaystyle(f,g,h)\colon(H,\Gamma,\Delta,\varphi)\to(H^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})

induces a homomorphism of groups

\displaystyle H\ast_{\Gamma}\Delta\to H^{\prime}\ast_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\Delta^{\prime}.

For future use, we also remark that if
X
is a set equipped with a right action of
H\rtimes\Delta
, then the action descends to the an action of
H\ast_{\Gamma}\Delta
on the quotient
X/\Gamma
, where here
\Gamma
acts on
X
via

(3.2)

x\cdot\gamma=x\cdot(\psi(\gamma),\varphi(\gamma)^{-1}).

3.2.2.
The groups
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})
and
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})^{\circ}

Let us now recall some notation from
[
8
]
.

If
{\mathbf{G}}
is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
, then we denote by
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
the connected component of the identity in
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the real topology. For a subgroup
\mathsf{H}
of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote by
\mathsf{H}_{+}
the inverse image of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
in
\mathsf{H}
. We write also
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}={\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})\cap{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
.

Now suppose
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
is a Shimura datum. Let
\mathbf{Z}
denote the center of
{\mathbf{G}}
, and let
\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}
denote the closure of
\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})
in
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. We define

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})\vcentcolon={\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}\ast_{{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})}{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+},

where
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
acts by conjugation on
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}
and
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})\to{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}
and
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})\to{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
are the obvious maps.

By
[
35
, Proposition 5.1]
, the map
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\to{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
is surjective with kernel
\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{R})\cap{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
, which is contained in the center of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
, so the conjugation action of
{\mathbf{G}}
on itself induces an action of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
on
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
. Combining this with the action of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
on
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
determines a right action of
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})
on
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{X})
.

Denote by
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}^{-}
the closure of
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}
in
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Define

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})^{\circ}={\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}^{-}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}\ast_{{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})}{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+},

where
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
acts by conjugation on
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}^{-}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}
and
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})\to{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}^{-}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}
and
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Q})_{+}/\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q})\to{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
are the obvious maps. Evidently
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})^{\circ}
is a subgroup of
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})
. But, the group
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})^{\circ}
depends only on
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{der}}
and not on
{\mathbf{G}}
, since by
[
8
, 2.1.15]
it is given by the completion of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
with respect to the topology whose open sets are images of congruence subgroups in
{\mathbf{G}}^{\textup{der}}(\mathbb{Q})
.

It is clear that both the associations
\mathbf{G}\mapsto\mathscr{A}(\mathbf{G})
and
\mathbf{G}\mapsto\mathscr{A}(\mathbf{G})^{\circ}
are functorial in maps of reductive groups
f\colon\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{H}
such that
f(Z(\mathbf{G}))\subseteq Z(\mathbf{H})
.

3.2.3.
The groups
\mathscr{A}(\bm{\mathcal{G}})
and
\mathscr{A}(\bm{\mathcal{G}})^{\circ}

We define also analogs of
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})
and
\mathscr{A}({\mathbf{G}})^{\circ}
for
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-valued points, following
[
26
, §4.5.6]
(see also
[
29
, §5.2.2]
).

To begin, we record the following well-known Beauville–Lazslo-type lemma (which is a largely basic case of
[
47
,
Tag 0F9Q
]
). For a ring
A
, let us denote by
\mathbf{AlgGrp}_{A}=\mathbf{AlgGrp}_{A}^{{\varnothing}}
the category of finite type affine group
A
-schemes, and by
\mathbf{AlgGrp}^{\mathrm{fl}}_{A}
and
\mathbf{AlgGrp}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{A}
the full subcategory of
A
-flat and
A
-smooth objects, respectively.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
R
be a Noetherian ring and
f
a non-zerodivisor of
R
. Denote the
f
-adic completion of
R
by
\widehat{R}
. Then the functor

\mathbf{AlgGrp}^{P}_{R}\to\mathbf{AlgGrp}^{P}_{R[\nicefrac{{1}}{{f}}]}\times_{\mathbf{AlgGrp}^{P}_{\widehat{R}[\nicefrac{{1}}{{f}}]}}\mathbf{AlgGrp}^{P}_{\widehat{R}},\quad\mathcal{H}\mapsto(\mathcal{H}_{R[\nicefrac{{1}}{{f}}]},\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{R}},\theta_{\mathrm{nat}}),

is an equivalence, where the target is the
2
-fiber product
(
with the projection maps the obvious base change
)
,
\theta_{\mathrm{nat}}
is the natural isomorphism, and
P\in\{{{\varnothing}},\mathrm{fl},\mathrm{sm}\}
.

Proof.

Observe that
\textup{Spec}\,(\widehat{R})\to\textup{Spec}\,(R)
is flat (e.g., see
[
47
,
Tag 00MB
]
) with image precisely the vanishing locus
V(f)
of
f
(e.g., as follows from combining
[
47
,
Tag 00MC
]
and
[
47
,
Tag 00HP
]
). Thus,

\textup{Spec}\,(\widehat{R})\sqcup\textup{Spec}\,(R[\nicefrac{{1}}{{f}}])\to\textup{Spec}\,(R)

is an fpqc cover. Moreover,

\textup{Spec}\,(\widehat{R})\times_{\textup{Spec}\,(R)}\textup{Spec}\,(R[\nicefrac{{1}}{{f}}])\simeq\textup{Spec}\,(\widehat{R}[\nicefrac{{1}}{{f}}]).

The fully faithfulness follows from
[
47
,
Tag 023Q
]
. The essential surjectivity follows from
[
47
,
Tag 0245
]
to descend to an affine scheme,
[
47
,
Tag 023Q
]
to descend the multiplication map and identity section, and finally
[
47
,
Tag 02KZ
]
,
[
47
,
Tag 02L2
]
, and
[
47
,
Tag 02VL
]
to descend the property
P
.
∎

Suppose now that that
\mathbf{G}
is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathcal{G}
is a parahoric model of
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{\textup{ad}}
denote the parahoric
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-model of
G^{\textup{ad}}
as discussed in §
2.1.5
. By Proposition
3.14
there exists a unique morphism of smooth group
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-schemes
\bm{\mathcal{G}}\to\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathrm{ad}}
modeling
\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}^{\mathrm{ad}}
and
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{ad}}
. Let
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}
denote the center of
{\mathbf{G}}
, and denote by
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}
the closure of
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}
in
\bm{\mathcal{G}}
.

Let
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{-}
and
\bm{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})_{+}^{-}
denote the closures of
\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
and
\bm{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})_{+}
in
{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
, respectively. Following
[
26
, §4.5.6]
and
[
29
, §5.2.2]
, we set

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}(\bm{\mathcal{G}})\vcentcolon={\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})/\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{-}\ast_{\bm{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})_{+}/\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})}\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{+},

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}(\bm{\mathcal{G}})^{\circ}\vcentcolon=\bm{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})_{+}^{-}/\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{-}\ast_{\bm{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})_{+}/\bm{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})}\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{\textup{ad}}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{+},

where in the first definition we have that
